[both the knaves and the people who buy first, and]{.s-t1} 

[the stress, often increased by fatigue, augments the targets\' susceptibility to the social proof. And, of]{.s-t1} 

[course, the techniques of our worst \"religious\" cults]{.s-t1} 

[imitate those of the knavish salesmen. One cult]{.s-t1} 

[even used rattlesnakes to heighten the stress felt by]{.s-t1} 

[conversion targets.]{.s-t1} 

 

[Because both bad and good behavior are made]{.s-t1} 

[contagious by Social-Proof Tendency, it is highly]{.s-t1} 

[important that human societies (1) stop any bad behavior before it spreads and (2) foster and display]{.s-t1} 

[all good behavior.]{.s-t1} 

 

[My father once told me that just after]{.s-t1} 

[commencing law practice in Omaha, he went with]{.s-t1} 

[a large group from Nebraska to South Dakota to hunt pheasants. A South Dakota hunting license]{.s-t1} 

[was, say, \$2 for South Dakota residents and \$5 for nonresidents. All the Nebraska residents, one]{.s-t1} 

[by one, signed up for South Dakota licenses with phony South Dakota addresses until it was my father\'s turn.]{.s-t1} 

[Then, according to him, he barely]{.s-t1} 

[prevented himself from doing what the others were]{.s-t1} 

[doing, which was some sort of criminal offense.]{.s-t1} 

 

[Not everyone so resists the social contagion of]{.s-t1} 

[bad behavior. And, therefore, we often get \"Serpico]{.s-t1} 

[Syndrome,\" named to commemorate the state of a near-totally corrupt New York police division joined]{.s-t1} 

[by Frank Serpico. He was then nearly murdered by gunfire because of his resistance to going along with]{.s-t1} 

[the corruption in the division. Such corruption was]{.s-t1} 

[being driven by social proof plus incentives, the combination that creates Serpico Syndrome. The Serpico story should be taught more than it now is]{.s-t1} 

[because the didactic power of its horror is aimed at]{.s-t1} 

[a very important evil, driven substantially by a very]{.s-t1} 

[important force: social proof.]{.s-t1} 

 

[In social proof, it is not only action by others that misleads but also their inaction. In the]{.s-t1} 

[presence of doubt, inaction by others becomes]{.s-t1} 

[social proof that inaction is the right course. Thus,]{.s-t1} 

[the inaction of a great many bystanders led to the death of Kitty Genovese in a famous incident much]{.s-t1} 

[discussed in introductory psychology courses.]{.s-t1} 

 

[In the ambit of social proof, the outside]{.s-t1} 

[directors on a corporate board usually display the near ultimate form of inaction. They fail to object to anything much short of an axe murder until some]{.s-t1} 

[public embarrassment of the board finally causes]{.s-t1} 

[their intervention. A typical board-of-directors\' culture was once well described by my friend, Joe Rosenfield, as he said, \"They asked me if I wanted to become a director of Northwest Bell, and it was]{.s-t1} 

[the last thing they ever asked me.\"]{.s-t1} 

 

[In advertising and sales promotion, Social-Proof]{.s-t1} 

[Tendency is about as strong a factor as one could]{.s-t1} 

[imagine. \"Monkey-see, monkey-do\" is the old phrase that reminds one of how strongly John will]{.s-t1} 

[often wish to do something, or have something, just because Joe does or has it. One interesting consequence is that an advertiser will pay a lot to have]{.s-t1} 

[its soup can, instead of someone else\'s, in a movie scene involving soup consumption only in a peripheral way.]{.s-t1} 

 

[Social-Proof Tendency often interacts in a perverse way with Envy/Jealousy and Deprival]{.s-t1} 

[Super Reaction Tendency. One such interaction]{.s-t1} 

[amused my family for years as people recalled the time when my cousin Russ and I, at ages three]{.s-t1} 

[and four, fought and howled over a single surplus]{.s-t1} 

[shingle while surrounded by a virtual sea of surplus]{.s-t1} 

[shingles.]{.s-t1} 

 

[But the adult versions of this occasion, boosted]{.s-t1} 

[by psychological tendencies preserving ideologies,]{.s-t1} 

[are not funny and can bring down whole civilizations. The Middle East now presents just such a threat. By now the resources spent by Jews, Arabs and all others over a small amount of disputed land]{.s-t1} 

[if divided arbitrarily among land claimants, would]{.s-t1} 

[have made everyone better off, even before taking]{.s-t1} 

[into account any benefit from reduced threat of war,]{.s-t1} 

[possibly nuclear.]{.s-t1} 

 

[Outside domestic relations it is rare now to try to resolve disputes by techniques including discussion of impacts from psychological tendencies.]{.s-t1} 

[Considering the implications of childishness that]{.s-t1} 

[would be raised by such inclusion, and the defects]{.s-t1} 

[of psychology as now taught, this result may be]{.s-t1} 

[sound. But, given the nuclear stakes now involved and the many failures in important negotiations lasting decades, I often wonder if some day, in some]{.s-t1} 

[way, more use of psychological insight will eventually improve outcomes. If so, correct teaching of psychology matters a lot. And, if old psychology]{.s-t1} 

[professors are even less likely than old physics]{.s-t1} 

[professors to learn new ways, which seems nearly]{.s-t1} 

[certain, then we may, as Max Planck predicted,]{.s-t1} 

[need a new generation of psychology professors]{.s-t1} 

[who have grown up to think in a different way.]{.s-t1} 

 

[If only one lesson is to be chosen from a package of lessons involving Social-Proof Tendency,]{.s-t1} 

[and used in self improvement, my favorite would]{.s-t1} 

[be: Learn how to ignore the examples from others]{.s-t1} 

[when they are wrong, because few skills are more]{.s-t1} 

[worth having.]{.s-t1} 

 

[Sixteen: Contrast-Misreaction Tendency]{.s-t4} 

 

[Because the nervous system of man does not naturally measure in absolute scientific units, it must instead rely on something simpler. The eyes]{.s-t1} 

[have a solution that limits their programming needs:]{.s-t1} 

[the contrast in what is seen is registered. And as in sight, so does it go, largely, in the other senses.]{.s-t1} 

[Moreover as perception goes, so goes cognition. The result is man\'s Contrast-MisReaction Tendency.]{.s-t1} 

 

[Few psychological tendencies do more damage to correct thinking. Small-scale damages involve]{.s-t1} 

[instances such as man\'s buying an overpriced]{.s-t1} 

[\$1,000 leather dashboard merely because the price is so low compared to his concurrent purchase of]{.s-t1} 

[a \$65,000 car. Large- scale damages often ruin]{.s-t1} 

[lives, as when a wonderful woman having terrible parents marries a man who would be judged satisfactory only in comparison to her parents. Or as]{.s-t1} 

[when a man takes wife number two who would be]{.s-t1} 

[appraised as all right only in comparison to wife]{.s-t1} 

[number one.]{.s-t1} 

 

[A particularly reprehensible form of sales]{.s-t1} 

[practice occurs in the offices of some real estate]{.s-t1} 

[brokers. A buyer from out of the city, perhaps]{.s-t1} 

[needing to shift his family there, visits the office with little time available. The salesman deliberately shows the customer three awful houses at]{.s-t1} 

[ridiculously high prices. Then he shows him a]{.s-t1} 

[merely bad house at a price only moderately too high. And, boom, the broker often makes an easy]{.s-t1} 

[sale.]{.s-t1} 

 

[Contrast-Misreaction Tendency is routinely]{.s-t1} 

[used to cause disadvantage for customers buying]{.s-t1} 

[merchandise and services. To make an ordinary price seem low, the vendor will very frequently]{.s-t1} 

[create a highly artificial price that is much higher]{.s-t1} 

[than the price always sought, then advertise his]{.s-t1} 

[standard price as a big reduction from his phony price. Even when people know that this sort of]{.s-t1} 

[customer manipulation is being attempted, it will]{.s-t1} 

[often work to trigger buying. This phenomenon]{.s-t1} 

[accounts in part for much advertising in newspapers. It also demonstrates that being aware of]{.s-t1} 

[psychological ploys is not a perfect defense.]{.s-t1} 

 

[When a man\'s steps are consecutively taken]{.s-t1} 

[toward disaster, with each step being very small, the brain\'s Contrast-Misreaction Tendency will often]{.s-t1} 

[let the man go too far toward disaster to be able to avoid it. This happens because each step presents so small a contrast from his present position.]{.s-t1} 

 

[A bridge-playing pal of mine once told me that a frog tossed into very hot water would jump out, but that the same frog would end up dying if]{.s-t1} 

[placed in room-temperature water that was later]{.s-t1} 

[heated at a very slow rate. My few shreds of physiological knowledge make me doubt this account. But no matter because many businesses die in just the manner claimed by - friend for the frog. Cognition, misled by tiny changes involving low]{.s-t1} 

[contrast, will often miss a trend that is destiny.]{.s-t1} 

 

[One of Ben Franklin\'s best-remembered and most useful aphorisms is \"A small leak will sink a great ship.\" The utility of the aphorism is large]{.s-t1} 

[precisely because the brain so often misses the]{.s-t1} 

[functional equivalent of a small leak in a great ship.]{.s-t1} 

 

[Seventeen: Stress- Influence Tendency]{.s-t4} 

 

[Everyone recognizes that sudden stress, for instance from a threat, will cause a rush of adrena]{.s-t1} 

[line in the human body, prompting faster and more]{.s-t1} 

[extreme reaction. And everyone who has taken]{.s-t1} 

[Psych 101 knows that stress makes Social-Proof]{.s-t1} 

[Tendency more powerful.]{.s-t1} 

 

[In a phenomenon less well recognized but still]{.s-t1} 

[widely known, light stress can slightly improve]{.s-t1} 

[performance-say, in examinations-whereas heavy]{.s-t1} 

[stress causes dysfunction.]{.s-t1} 

 

[But few people know more about really heavy]{.s-t1} 

[stress than that it can cause depression. For]{.s-t1} 

[instance, most people know that an \"acute stress]{.s-t1} 

[depression\" makes thinking dysfunctional because]{.s-t1} 

[it causes an extreme of pessimism, often extended]{.s-t1} 

[in length and usually accompanied by activity]{.s-t1} 

[stopping fatigue. Fortunately, as most people also]{.s-t1} 

[know, such a depression is one of mankind\'s more]{.s-t1} 

[reversible ailments. Even before modern drugs]{.s-t1} 

[were available, many people afflicted by depression,]{.s-t1} 

[such as Winston Churchill and Samuel Johnson,]{.s-t1} 

[gained great achievement in life.]{.s-t1} 

 

[Most people know very little about nondepressive mental breakdowns influenced by heavy]{.s-t1} 

[stress. But there is at least one exception, involving]{.s-t1} 

[the work of Pavlov when he was in his seventies]{.s-t1} 

[and eighties. Pavlov had won a Nobel Prize early]{.s-t1} 

[in life by using dogs to work out the physiology of digestion. Then he became world-famous by]{.s-t1} 

[working out mere-association responses in dogs,]{.s-t1} 

[initially salivating dogs-so much so that changes in]{.s-t1} 

[behavior triggered by mere-association, like those]{.s-t1} 

[caused by much modern advertisement, are today]{.s-t1} 

[often said to come from \"Pavlovian\" conditioning.]{.s-t1} 

 

[What happened to cause Pavlov\'s last work was]{.s-t1} 

[especially interesting. During the great Leningrad]{.s-t1} 

[Flood of the 1920s, Pavlov had many dogs in cages. Their habits had been transformed, by a combination of his \"Pavlovian conditioning\" plus standard]{.s-t1} 

[reward responses, into distinct and different]{.s-t1} 

[patterns. As the waters of the flood came up and]{.s-t1} 

[receded, many dogs reached a point where they had]{.s-t1} 

[almost no airspace between their noses and the tops of their cages. This subjected them to maximum]{.s-t1} 

[stress. Immediately thereafter, Pavlov noticed]{.s-t1} 

[that many of the dogs were no longer behaving as they had. The dog that formerly had liked his trainer now disliked him, for example. This result]{.s-t1} 

[reminds one of modern cognition reversals in which]{.s-t1} 

[a person\'s love of his parents suddenly becomes]{.s-t1} 

[hate, as new love has been shifted suddenly to a cult. The unanticipated, extreme changes in]{.s-t1} 

[Pavlov\'s dogs would have driven any good experimental scientist into a near-frenzy of curiosity. That]{.s-t1} 

[was indeed Pavlov\'s reaction. But not many scientists would have done what Pavlov next did.]{.s-t1} 

 

[And that was to spend the rest of his long life]{.s-t1} 

[giving stress-induced nervous breakdowns to dogs,]{.s-t1} 

[after which he would try to reverse the break]{.s-t1} 

[downs, all the while keeping careful experimental records. He found (1) that he could classify dogs]{.s-t1} 

[so as to predict how easily a particular dog would breakdown (2) that the dogs hardest to break]{.s-t1} 

[down were also the hardest to return to their pre]{.s-t1} 

[breakdown state; (3) that any dog could be broken]{.s-t1} 

[down; and (4) that he couldn\'t reverse a breakdown]{.s-t1} 

[except by reimposing stress.]{.s-t1} 

 

[Now, practically everyone is revolted by such]{.s-t1} 

[experimental treatment of man\'s friend, the dog.]{.s-t1} 

[Moreover, Pavlov was Russian and did his last]{.s-t1} 

[work under the Communists. And maybe those]{.s-t1} 

[facts account for the present extreme, widespread ignorance of Pavlov\'s last work. The two Freudian psychiatrists with whom I tried many years ago to discuss this work had never heard of it. And]{.s-t1} 

[the dean of a major medical school actually asked]{.s-t1} 

[me, several years ago, if any of Pavlov\'s experiments were \"repeatable\" in experiments of other researchers. Obviously, Pavlov is now a sort of]{.s-t1} 

[forgotten hero in medical science.]{.s-t1} 

 

[I first found a description of Pavlov\'s last work in a popular paperback, written by some Rockefeller-financed psychiatrist, when I was trying to figure out (1) how cults worked their horrible mischief and (2) what should the law say about what parents could do to \"deprogram\" children who had]{.s-t1} 

[become brainwashed zombies. Naturally, main]{.s-t1} 

[stream law objected to the zombies being physically]{.s-t1} 

[captured by their parents and next subjected to stress that would help to deprogram the effects of the stress they had endured in cult conversions.]{.s-t1} 

 

[I never wanted to get into the legal controversy that existed about this subject. But I did conclude that the controversy couldn\'t be handled with maximized rationality without considering whether,]{.s-t1} 

[as Pavlov\'s last work suggests, the heavy-handed imposition of stress might be the only reversal method that would work to remedy one of the worst evils imaginable: a stolen mind. I have included this discussion of Pavlov (1) partly out of general antagonism toward taboos, (2) partly to make my talk reasonably complete as it considers stress and (3) partly because I hope some listener may]{.s-t1} 

[continue my inquiry with more success.]{.s-t1} 

 

[Eighteen: Availability- Mis Weighing Tendency]{.s-t4} 

 

[This mental tendency echoes the words of the song: \"When I\'m not near the girl I love, I love the girl I\'m near.\" Man\'s imperfect, limited-capacity]{.s-t1} 

[brain easily drifts into working with what\'s easily available to it. And the brain can\'t use what it can\'t remember or what it is blocked from recognizing because it is heavily influenced by one or more]{.s-t1} 

[psychological tendencies bearing strongly on it, as the fellow is influenced by the nearby girl in the]{.s-t1} 

[song. And so the mind overweighs what is easily]{.s-t1} 

[available and thus displays Availability- Mis Weighing]{.s-t1} 

[Tendency.]{.s-t1} 

 

[The main antidote to miscues from Availability]{.s-t1} 

[Misweighing Tendency often involve procedures, including use of checklists, which are almost always helpful.]{.s-t1} 

 

[Another antidote is to behave somewhat like Darwin did when he emphasized disconfirming]{.s-t1} 

[evidence. What should be done is to especially]{.s-t1} 

[emphasize factors that don\'t produce reams of easily]{.s-t1} 

[available numbers, instead of drifting mostly or entirely into considering factors that do produce]{.s-t1} 

[such numbers. Still another antidote is to find and]{.s-t1} 

[hire some skeptical, articulate people with far]{.s-t1} 

[reaching minds to act as advocates for notions that]{.s-t1} 

[are opposite to the incumbent notions.]{.s-t1} 

 

[One consequence of this tendency is that extra vivid evidence, being so memorable and thus more]{.s-t1} 

[available in cognition, should often consciously be]{.s-t1} 

[underweighted while less vivid evidence should be]{.s-t1} 

[overweighed.]{.s-t1} 

 

[Still, the special strength of extra-vivid images]{.s-t1} 

[in influencing the mind can be constructively used (1) in persuading someone else to reach a]{.s-t1} 

[correct conclusion or (2) as a device for improving]{.s-t1} 

[one\'s own memory by attaching vivid images, one]{.s-t1} 

[after the other, to many items one doesn\'t want to forget. Indeed, such use of vivid images as]{.s-t1} 

[memory boosters is what enabled the great orators]{.s-t1} 

[of classical Greece and Rome to give such long,]{.s-t1} 

[organized speeches without using notes.]{.s-t1} 

 

[The great algorithm to remember in dealing with]{.s-t1} 

[this tendency is simple: An idea or a fact is not worth more merely because it is easily available to you.]{.s-t1} 

 

[Nineteen: Use-It-or-Lose-It Tendency]{.s-t4} 

 

[All skills attenuate with disuse. I was a whiz]{.s-t1} 

[at calculus until age twenty, after which the skill was soon obliterated by total nonuse. The right antidote to such a loss is to make use of the functional equivalent of the aircraft simulator employed in pilot training. This allows a pilot to continuously practice all of the rarely used skills that he can\'t afford to lose.]{.s-t1} 

 

[Throughout his life, a wise man engages in]{.s-t1} 

[practice of all his useful, rarely used skills, many]{.s-t1} 

[of them outside his discipline, as a sort of duty to his better self. If he reduces the number of skills]{.s-t1} 

[he practices and, therefore, the number of skills he retains, he will naturally drift into error from man with a hammer tendency. His learning capacity will also shrink as he creates gaps in the latticework of]{.s-t1} 

[theory he needs as a framework for understanding new experience. It is also essential for a thinking]{.s-t1} 

[man to assemble his skills into a checklist that he]{.s-t1} 

[routinely uses. Any other mode of operation ]{.s-t1}[w]{.s-t7}[ill]{.s-t1} 

[cause him to miss much that is important.]{.s-t1} 

 

[Skills of a very high order can be maintained]{.s-t1} 

[only with daily practice. The pianist Paderewski]{.s-t1} 

[once said that if he failed to practice for a single day,]{.s-t1} 

[he could notice his performance deterioration and]{.s-t1} 

[that, after a week\'s gap in practice, the audience]{.s-t1} 

[could notice it as well.]{.s-t1} 

 

[The hard rule of Lose-It-or-Lose-It Tendency tempers its harshness for the diligent. If a skill is]{.s-t1} 

[raised to fluency, instead of merely being crammed]{.s-t1} 

[in briefly to enable one to pass some test, then the skill (1) will be lost more slowly and (2) will come]{.s-t1} 

[back faster when refreshed with new learning.]{.s-t1} 

[These are not minor advantages, and a wise man engaged in learning some important skill will not]{.s-t1} 

[stop until he is really fluent in it.]{.s-t1} 

[]{#index_split_000.xhtml_anchor29 .calibre2} 

[Twenty: Drug-Misinfluence Tendency]{.s-t14} 

 

[This tendency\'s destructive power is so widely]{.s-t1} 

[known to be intense, with frequent tragic consequences for cognition and the outcome of life, that]{.s-t1} 

[it needs no discussion here to supplement that]{.s-t1} 

[previously given under \"Simple, Pain-Avoiding]{.s-t1} 

[Psychological Denial.\"]{.s-t1} 

 

[Twenty-One: Senescence-Miss Influence Tendency]{.s-t4} 

 

[With advanced age, there comes a natural]{.s-t1} 

[cognitive decay, differing among individuals in the]{.s-t1} 

[earliness of its arrival and the speed of its progression. Practically no one is good at learning complex]{.s-t1} 

[new skills when very old. But some people remain]{.s-t1} 

[pretty good in maintaining intensely practiced old]{.s-t1} 

[skills until late in life. as one can notice in many a]{.s-t1} 

[bridge tournament.]{.s-t1} 

 

[Old people like me get pretty skilled, without]{.s-t1} 

[working at it, at disguising age-related deterioraion because social convention, like clothing, hides]{.s-t1} 

[much decline.]{.s-t1} 

 

[Continuous thinking and learning, done with joy can somewhat help delay what is inevitable.]{.s-t1} 

 

[Twenty-TWo: Authority-Misinfluence Tendency]{.s-t4} 

 

[Living in dominance hierarchies as he does, like]{.s-t1} 

[all his ancestors before him, man was born mostly]{.s-t1} 

[to follow leaders, with only a few people doing]{.s-t1} 

[the leading. And so, human society is formally]{.s-t1} 

[organized into dominance hierarchies, with their]{.s-t1} 

[culture augmenting the natural follow-the-leader]{.s-t1} 

[tendency of man.]{.s-t1} 

 

[But automatic as most human reactions are, with]{.s-t1} 

[the tendency to follow leaders being no exception,]{.s-t1} 

[man is often destined to suffer greatly when the]{.s-t1} 

[leader is wrong or when his leader\'s ideas don\'t get]{.s-t1} 

[through properly in the bustle of life and are misunderstood. And so, we find much miscognition from]{.s-t1} 

[man\'s Authority-Mis Influence Tendency.]{.s-t1} 

 

[Some of the mis influences are amusing, as in]{.s-t1} 

[a case described by Cialdini. A physician left a written order for a nurse treating an earache, as follows: \"Tho drops, twice a day, rear.\"\' The]{.s-t1} 

[nurse then directed the patient to turn over and put]{.s-t1} 

[the eardrops in his anus.]{.s-t1} 

 

[Other versions of confused instructions from authority figures are tragic. In World War II, a new pilot for a general, who sat beside him in the]{.s-t1} 

[copilot\'s seat, was so anxious to please his boss]{.s-t1} 

[that he misinterpreted some minor shift in the]{.s-t1} 

[general\'s position as a direction to do some foolish thing. The pilot crashed the plane and became a]{.s-t1} 

[paraplegic.]{.s-t1} 

 

[Well, naturally, cases like this one get the]{.s-t1} 

[attention of careful thinkers like Boss Buffett, who]{.s-t1} 

[always acts like an over quiet mouse around his]{.s-t1} 

[pilots.]{.s-t1} 

 

[Such cases are also given attention in the]{.s-t1} 

[simulator training of copilots who have to learn to]{.s-t1} 

[ignore certain really foolish orders from boss pilots because boss pilots will sometimes err disastrously.]{.s-t1} 

[Even after going through such a training regime,]{.s-t1} 

[however, copilots in simulator exercises will too]{.s-t1} 

[often allow the simulated plane to crash because of]{.s-t1} 

[some extreme and perfectly obvious simulated error]{.s-t1} 

[of the chief pilot.]{.s-t1} 

 

[After Corporal Hitler had risen to dominance]{.s-t1} 

[Germany, leading a bunch of believing Lutherans]{.s-t1} 

[and Catholics into orgies of genocide and other]{.s-t1} 

[mass destruction, one clever psychology professor,]{.s-t1} 

[Stanley Milgram, decided to do an experiment to]{.s-t1} 

[determine exactly how far authority figures could]{.s-t1} 

[lead ordinary people into gross misbehavior. In this]{.s-t1} 

[experiment, a man posing as an authority figure,]{.s-t1} 

[namely a professor governing a respectable experiment, was able to trick a great many ordinary people]{.s-t1} 

[into giving what they had every reason to believe]{.s-t1} 

[were massive electric shocks that inflicted heavy]{.s-t1} 

[torture on innocent fellow citizens. This experiment did demonstrate a terrible result contributed]{.s-t1} 

[to by Authority-Misinfluence Tendency, but it also]{.s-t1} 

[demonstrated extreme ignorance in the psychology professoriate right after World War II.]{.s-t1} 

 

[Almost any intelligent person with my checklist]{.s-t1} 

[of psychological tendencies in his hand would, by]{.s-t1} 

[simply going down the checklist, have seen that Milgram\'s experiment involved about six powerful]{.s-t1} 

[psychological tendencies acting in confluence to]{.s-t1} 

[bring about his extreme experimental result. For]{.s-t1} 

[instance, the person pushing Milgram\' s shock]{.s-t1} 

[lever was given much social proof from presence]{.s-t1} 

[of inactive bystanders whose silence communicated that his behavior was okay. Yet it took over]{.s-t1} 

[a thousand psychological papers, published before]{.s-t1} 

[I got to Milgram, for the professoriate to get his experiment only about ninety percent as well]{.s-t1} 

[understood as it would have immediately been by any intelligent person who used (1) any sensible]{.s-t1} 

[organization of psychology along the lines of this talk, plus (2) a checklist procedure. This outcome]{.s-t1} 

[displaying the dysfunctional thinking of long-dead professors deserves a better explanation. I will later]{.s-t1} 

[deal with the subject in a very hesitant fashion.]{.s-t1} 

 

[We can be pleased that the psychology professoriate of a former era wasn\'t quite as dysfunctional as the angler in my next-to-last illustration of]{.s-t1} 

[Authority-Mis influence Tendency.]{.s-t1} 

 

[When I once fished in the Rio Colorado in Costa Rica, my guide, in a state of shock, told me a]{.s-t1} 

[story about an angler who\'d earlier come to the river]{.s-t1} 

[without ever having fished for tarpon. A fishing guide like the one I had runs the boat and gives fishing advice, establishing himself in this context as the ultimate authority figure. In the case of]{.s-t1} 

[this guide, his native language was Spanish, while the angler\'s native language was English. The angler got a big tarpon on and began submitting to]{.s-t1} 

[many directions from this authority figure called a guide: tip up, tip down, reel in, etc. Finally, when it was necessary to put more pressure on the fish]{.s-t1} 

[by causing more bending of the angler\'s rod, the]{.s-t1} 

[guide said in English: \"Give him the rod, give him the rod.\" Well, the angler threw his expensive rod at the fish, and when last seen, it was going down the Rio Colorado toward the ocean. This example]{.s-t1} 

[shows how powerful is the tendency to go along]{.s-t1} 

[with an authority figure and how it can turn one\'s]{.s-t1} 

[brain into mush.]{.s-t1} 

 

[My final example comes from business. A]{.s-t1} 

[psychology Ph. D. once became a CEO of a major]{.s-t1} 

[company and went wild, creating an expensive new]{.s-t1} 

[headquarters, with a great wine cellar, at an isolated]{.s-t1} 

[site. At some point, his underlings remonstrated that money was running short. \"Take the money]{.s-t1} 

[out of the depreciation reserves,\" said the CEO.]{.s-t1} 

[Not too easy because a depreciation reserve is a]{.s-t1} 

[liability account.]{.s-t1} 

 

[So strong is undue respect for authority that]{.s-t1} 

[this CEO, and many even worse examples, have actually been allowed to remain in control of important business institutions for long periods after it was clear they should be removed. The obvious]{.s-t1} 

[implication: Be careful who you appoint to power because a dominant authority figure will often be hard to remove, aided as he will be by Authority]{.s-t1} 

[Misinfluence Tendency.]{.s-t1} 

 

[Twenty-Three: Twaddle Tendency]{.s-t1} 

 

[Man, as a social animal who has the gift of language, is born to prattle and to pour out twaddle]{.s-t1} 

[that does much damage when serious work is being attempted. Some people produce copious amounts]{.s-t1} 

[of twaddle and others very little.]{.s-t1} 

 

[A trouble from the honeybee version of twaddle]{.s-t1} 

[was once demonstrated in an interesting experiment. A honeybee normally goes out and finds]{.s-t1} 

[nectar and then comes back and does a dance that]{.s-t1} 

[communicates to the other bees where the nectar is. The other bees then go out and get it. Well some scientist-clever, like B. F, Skinner-decided to see how well a honeybee would do with a handicap. He put the nectar straight up. Way up. Well, in a]{.s-t1} 

[natural setting, there is no nectar a long way straight]{.s-t1} 

[up, and the poor honeybee doesn\'t have a generic]{.s-t1} 

[program that is adequate to handle what she now]{.s-t1} 

[has to communicate. You might guess that this]{.s-t1} 

[honeybee would come back to the hive and slink into a corner, but she doesn\'t. She comes into the hive and does an incoherent dance. Well, all my life I\'ve been dealing with the human equivalent of that honeybee. And it\'s a very important part of wise administration to keep prattling people, pouring our twaddle, far away from the serious work. A rightly]{.s-t1} 

[famous Caltech engineering professor, exhibiting]{.s-t1} 

[more insight than tact, once expressed his version of this idea as follows: \"The principal job of an]{.s-t1} 

[academic administration is to keep the people who don\'t matter from interfering with the work of the people that do.\" I include this quotation partly because I long suffered from backlash caused]{.s-t1} 

[by my version of this professor\'s conversational manner. After much effort, I was able to improve]{.s-t1} 

[only slightly, so one of my reasons for supplying the quotation is my hope that, at least in comparison, I will appear tactful.]{.s-t1} 

 

[Twenty-Four: Reason-RespEcting Tendency]{.s-t1} 

 

[There is in man, particularly one in an advanced]{.s-t1} 

[culture, a natural love of accurate cognition and a joy in its exercise. This accounts for the widespread popularity of crossword puzzles, other puzzles,]{.s-t1} 

[and bridge and chess columns, as well as all games]{.s-t1} 

[requiring mental skill.]{.s-t1} 

 

[This tendency has an obvious implication. It]{.s-t1} 

[makes man especially prone to learn well when a]{.s-t1} 

[would-be teacher gives correct reasons for what]{.s-t1} 

[is taught, instead of simply laying out the desired belief ex cathedra with no reasons given. Few]{.s-t1} 

[practices, therefore, are wiser than not only thinking]{.s-t1} 

[through reasons before giving orders but also]{.s-t1} 

[communicating these reasons to the recipient of the]{.s-t1} 

[order.]{.s-t1} 

 

[No one knew this better than Carl Braun, who designed oil refineries with spectacular skill and integrity. He had a very simple rule, one of many]{.s-t1} 

[in his large, Teutonic company: You had to tell Who was to do What, Where, When, and Why. And if]{.s-t1} 

[you wrote a communication leaving out your explanation of why the addressee was to do what was]{.s-t1} 

[ordered, Braun was likely to fire you because Braun]{.s-t1} 

[well knew that ideas got through best when reasons]{.s-t1} 

[for the ideas were meticulously laid out.]{.s-t1} 

 

[In general, learning is most easily assimilated]{.s-t1} 

[and used when, life long, people consistently hang]{.s-t1} 

[their experience, actual and vicarious, on a lattice]{.s-t1} 

[work of theory answering the question: Why?]{.s-t1} 

[Indeed, the question \"Why?\" is a sort of Rosetta]{.s-t1} 

[stone opening up the major potentiality of mental]{.s-t1} 

[life.]{.s-t1} 

 

[Unfortunately, Reason- Respecting Tendency]{.s-t1} 

[is so strong that even a person\'s giving of meaningless or incorrect reasons will increase compliance with his orders and requests. This has been]{.s-t1} 

[demonstrated in psychology experiments wherein \"compliance practitioners\" successfully jump co]{.s-t1} 

[the head of the lines in front of copying machines by explaining their reason: \"I have to make some]{.s-t1} 

[copies.\" This sort of unfortunate byproduct of]{.s-t1} 

[Reason-Respecting Tendency is a conditioned]{.s-t1} 

[reflex, based on a widespread appreciation of the]{.s-t1} 

[importance of reasons. And, naturally, the practice]{.s-t1} 

[of laying out various claptrap reasons is much used]{.s-t1} 

[by commercial and cult \"compliance practitioners\"]{.s-t1} 

[to help them get what they don\'t deserve.]{.s-t1} 

 

[Twenty-Five: Lollapalooza Tendency-The Tendency to Get Extreme Consequences from Confluences of]{.s-t4} 

[Psychological Tendencies Acting in Favor of a Particular Outcome]{.s-t4} 

 

[This tendency was not in any of the psychology texts I once examined, at least in any coherent fashion, yet it dominates life. It accounts for the]{.s-t1} 

[extreme result in the Milgram experiment and the]{.s-t1} 

[extreme success of some cults that have stumbled]{.s-t1} 

[through practice evolution into bringing pressure]{.s-t1} 

[from many psychological tendencies to bear at the same time on conversion targets. The targets]{.s-t1} 

[vary in susceptibility, like the dogs Pavlov worked]{.s-t1} 

[with in his old age, but some of the minds that are]{.s-t1} 

[targeted simply snap into zombiedom under cult]{.s-t1} 

[pressure. Indeed, that is one cult\'s name for the]{.s-t1} 

[conversion phenomenon: snapping.]{.s-t1} 

 

[What are we to make of the extreme ignorance]{.s-t1} 

[of the psychology textbook writers of yesteryear?]{.s-t1} 

[How could anyone who had taken a freshman]{.s-t1} 

[course in physics or chemistry not be driven to]{.s-t1} 

[consider, above all, how psychological tendencies]{.s-t1} 

[combine and with what effects? Why would anyone]{.s-t1} 

[think his study of psychology was adequate without]{.s-t1} 

[his having endured the complexity involved in]{.s-t1} 

[dealing with intertwined psychological tendencies?]{.s-t1} 

[What could be more ironic than professors using]{.s-t1} 

[oversimplified notions while studying bad cognitive effects grounded in the mind\'s tendency to use]{.s-t1} 

[oversimplified algorithms?]{.s-t1} 

 

[I will make a few tentative suggestions. Maybe]{.s-t1} 

[many of the long-dead professors wanted to create]{.s-t1} 

[a whole science from one narrow type of repeatable psychology experiment that was conductible in a university setting and that aimed at one psychological tendency at a time. If so, these early]{.s-t1} 

[psychology professors made a massive error in so restricting their approach to their subject. It would be like physics ignoring (1) astrophysics because it couldn\'t happen in a physics lab, plus (2) all]{.s-t1} 

[compound effects. What psychological tendencies could account for early psychology professors]{.s-t1} 

[adopting an over-restricted approach to their own]{.s-t1} 

[subject matter? One candidate would be Avail]{.s-t1} 

[ability-Mis Weighing Tendency grounded in a]{.s-t1} 

[preference for easy-to-control data. And then the]{.s-t1} 

[restrictions would eventually create an extreme]{.s-t1} 

[case of man with a hammer tendency. Another]{.s-t1} 

[candidate might be envy/jealousy Tendency]{.s-t1} 

[through which early psychology professors]{.s-t1} 

[displayed some weird form of envy of a physics]{.s-t1} 

[that was misunderstood. And this possibility tends]{.s-t1} 

[to demonstrate that leaving envy/jealousy out of]{.s-t1} 

[academic psychology was never a good idea.]{.s-t1} 

 

[I now quitclaim all these historical mysteries to]{.s-t1} 

[my betters.]{.s-t1} 

 

[Well, that ends my brief description of psycho]{.s-t1} 

[logical tendencies.]{.s-t1} 

 

[Questions and Answers:]{.s-t4} 

 

[Now, as promised, I will ask and answer a few]{.s-t1} 

[general questions.]{.s-t1} 

 

[My first is a compound question: Isn\'t this list]{.s-t1} 

[of psychological tendencies tautological to some]{.s-t1} 

[extent compared to the system of Euclid? That]{.s-t1} 

[is, aren\'t there overlaps in the tendencies? And couldn\'t the system be laid out just as plausibly in a]{.s-t1} 

[somewhat different way? The answers are yes, yes,]{.s-t1} 

[and yes, but this matters only moderately. Further]{.s-t1} 

[refinement of these tendencies, while desirable, has]{.s-t1} 

[a limited practical potential because a significant]{.s-t1} 

[amount of messiness is unfixable in a soft science]{.s-t1} 

[like psychology.]{.s-t1} 

 

[My second question is: Can you supply a real]{.s-t1} 

[world model, instead of a Milgram-type controlled]{.s-t1} 

[psychology experiment, that uses your system]{.s-t1} 

[to illustrate multiple psychological tendencies]{.s-t1} 

[interacting in a plausibly diagnosable way? The answer is yes. One of my favorite cases involves]{.s-t1} 

[the McDonnell Douglas airliner evacuation test.]{.s-t1} 

[Before a new airliner can be sold, the government]{.s-t1} 

[requires that it pass an evacuation test, during]{.s-t1} 

[which a full load of passengers must get our in some]{.s-t1} 

[short period of time. The government directs that]{.s-t1} 

[the test be realistic. So you can\'t pass by evacuating only twenty-year-old athletes. So McDonnell]{.s-t1} 

[Douglas scheduled such a test in a darkened hangar using a lot of old people as evacuees. The]{.s-t1} 

[passenger cabin was, say, twenty feet above the]{.s-t1} 

[concrete floor of the hangar and was to be evacuated through moderately flimsy rubber chutes. The first]{.s-t1} 

[test was made in the morning. There were about]{.s-t1} 

[twenty very serious injuries, and the evacuation took so long it flunked the time test. So what did McDonnell Douglas next do? It repeated the test]{.s-t1} 

[in the afternoon, and this time there was another]{.s-t1} 

[failure, with about twenty more serious injuries,]{.s-t1} 

[including one case of permanent paralysis.]{.s-t1} 

 

[What psychological tendencies contributed to]{.s-t1} 

[this terrible result? Well, using my tendency list as a checklist, I come up with the following explanation. Reward-Super Response Tendency drove McDonnell Douglas to act fast. It couldn\'t sell its airliner until it passed the test. Also pushing]{.s-t1} 

[the company was Doubt-Avoidance Tendency]{.s-t1} 

[with its natural drive to arrive at a decision and run with it. Then the government\'s direction that the test be realistic drove Authority-Mis Influence]{.s-t1} 

[Tendency into the mischief of causing McDonnell]{.s-t1} 

[Douglas to overreact by using what was obviously]{.s-t1} 

[too dangerous a test method. By now the course]{.s-t1} 

[of action had been decided, so Inconsistency]{.s-t1} 

[Avoidance Tendency helped preserve the near]{.s-t1} 

[idiotic plan. When all the old people got to the]{.s-t1} 

[dark hangar, with its high airline cabin and concrete]{.s-t1} 

[floor, the situation must have made McDonnell]{.s-t1} 

[Douglas employees very queasy, but they saw other]{.s-t1} 

[employees and supervisors not objecting. Social]{.s-t1} 

[Proof Tendency, therefore, swamped the queasiness. And this allowed continued action as planned,]{.s-t1} 

[a continuation that was aided by more Authority]{.s-t1} 

[Misinfluence Tendency. Then came the disaster]{.s-t1} 

[of the morning test with its failure, plus serious]{.s-t1} 

[injuries. McDonnell Douglas ignored the strong]{.s-t1} 

[disconfirming evidence from the failure of the first]{.s-t1} 

[test because confirmation bias, aided by the triggering of strong Deprival-Super Reaction Tendency,]{.s-t1} 

[favored maintaining the original plan. McDonnell]{.s-t1} 

[Douglas\' Deprival-Super Reaction Tendency was]{.s-t1} 

[now like that which causes a gambler, bent on]{.s-t1} 

[getting even after a huge loss, to make his final]{.s-t1} 

[big bet. After all, McDonnell Douglas was going to lose a lot if it didn\'t pass its test as scheduled.]{.s-t1} 

[More psychology-based explanation can probably]{.s-t1} 

[be made, but the foregoing discussion is complete enough to demonstrate the utility of my system]{.s-t1} 

[when used in a checklist mode.]{.s-t1} 

 

[My third question is also compound: In the]{.s-t1} 

[practical world, what good is the thought system]{.s-t1} 

[laid out in this list of tendencies? Isn\'t practical]{.s-t1} 

[benefit prevented because these psychological tendencies are so thoroUghly programmed into the]{.s-t1} 

[human mind by broad evolution \[the combination of genetic and cultural evolution\] that we can\'t get]{.s-t1} 

[rid of them? Well, the answer is that the tendencies]{.s-t1} 

[are probably much more good than bad. Otherwise,]{.s-t1} 

[they wouldn\'t be there, working pretty well for]{.s-t1} 

[man, given his condition and his limited brain]{.s-t1} 

[capacity. So the tendencies can\'t be simply washed]{.s-t1} 

[out automatically, and shouldn\'t be. Nevertheless, the psychological thought system described,]{.s-t1} 

[when properly understood and used, enables the]{.s-t1} 

[spread of wisdom and good conduct and facilitates]{.s-t1} 

[the avoidance of disaster. Tendency is not always]{.s-t1} 

[destiny, and knowing the tendencies and their]{.s-t1} 

[antidotes can often help prevent trouble that would]{.s-t1} 

[otherwise occur. Here is a short list of examples reminding us of the great utility of elementary]{.s-t1} 

[psychological knowledge:]{.s-t1} 

 

[One: Carl Braun\'s communication practices.]{.s-t1} 

 

[Two: The use of simulators in pilot training.]{.s-t1} 

 

[Three: The system of Alcoholics Anonymous.]{.s-t1} 

 

[Four: Clinical training methods in medical schools.]{.s-t1} 

 

[Five: The rules of the U.S. Constitutional Convention: totally secret meetings, no recorded]{.s-t1} 

[vote by name until the final vote, votes reversible at any time before the end of the convention, then just]{.s-t1} 

[one vote on the whole Constitution. These are very clever psychology-respecting rules. If the founders had used a different procedure, many people would]{.s-t1} 

[have been pushed by various psychological tendencies into inconsistent, hardened positions. The]{.s-t1} 

[elite founders got our Constitution through by a]{.s-t1} 

[whisker only because they were psychologically]{.s-t1} 

[acute.]{.s-t1} 

 

[Six: The use of Granny\'s incentive-driven rule to manipulate oneself toward better performance of one\'s duties.]{.s-t1} 

 

[Seven: The Harvard Business School\'s]{.s-t1} 

[emphasis on decision trees. When I was young and foolish I used to laugh at the Harvard Business School. I said, \"They\'re teaching twenty-eight year-old people that high school algebra works in real life\" But later, I wised up and realized that it was very important that they do that to counter]{.s-t1} 

[some bad effects from psychological tendencies.]{.s-t1} 

[Better late than never.]{.s-t1} 

 

[Eight: The use of autopsy equivalents at Johnson & Johnson. At most corporations, if you make an acquisition and it turns out to be a]{.s-t1} 

[disaster, all the people, paperwork, and presentations that caused the foolish acquisition are quickly]{.s-t1} 

[forgotten. Nobody wants to be associated with the poor outcome by mentioning it. But at Johnson & Johnson, the rules make everybody revisit old acquisitions, comparing predictions with outcomes. That is a very smart thing to do.]{.s-t1} 

 

[Nine: The great example of Charles Darwin as]{.s-t1} 

[he avoided confirmation bias, which has morphed into the extreme anti-confirmation-bias method of the \"double blind\" studies wisely required in drug]{.s-t1} 

[research by the FDA]{.s-t1} 

 

[Ten: The Warren Buffett rule for open-outcry]{.s-t1} 

[auctions: Don\'t go.]{.s-t1} 

 

[My fourth question is; What special knowledge]{.s-t1} 

[problems lie buried in the thought system demon]{.s-t1} 

[started by your list?]{.s-t1} 

 

[Well, one answer is paradox. In social]{.s-t1} 

[psychology, the more people learn about the system]{.s-t1} 

[the less it is true, and this is what gives the system]{.s-t1} 

[its great value as a preventer of bad outcomes and a]{.s-t1} 

[driver of good outcomes. This result is paradoxical, and doesn\'t remind one of elementary physics, but]{.s-t1} 

[so what. One can\'t get all the paradox out of pure]{.s-t1} 

[math, so why should psychology be shocked by]{.s-t1} 

[some paradox?]{.s-t1} 

 

[There is also some paradox in cognition change]{.s-t1} 

[that works even when the manipulated person knows he is being manipulated. This creates a sort of paradox in a paradox, but, again, so what. I once much enjoyed an occasion of this sort. I drew this]{.s-t1} 

[beautiful woman as my dinner partner many years ago. I\'d never seen her before. She was married]{.s-t1} 

[to a prominent Los Angeles man. She sat down]{.s-t1} 

[next to me, turned her beautiful face up, and said,]{.s-t1} 

[\"Charlie, what one word accounts for your remarkable success in life?\" I knew I was being manipulated by a practiced routine, and I just loved it. I never see this woman without a little lift in my spirits. And, by the way, I told her I was rational. You\'ll have to judge yourself whether that\'s true. I]{.s-t1} 

[may be demonstrating some psychological tendency I hadn\'t planned on demonstrating.]{.s-t1} 

 

[My fifth question is: Don\'t we need more reconciliation of psychology and economics? My answer]{.s-t1} 

[is yes, and I suspect that some slight progress is being made. I have heard of one such example. Colin Camerer of Caltech, who works in \"experimental economics,\" devised an interesting experiment in which he caused high I.Q. students, playing for real money, to pay price A+B for a \"security\" they knew would turn into A dollars at the end of]{.s-t1} 

[the day. This foolish action occurred because the]{.s-t1} 

[students were allowed to trade with each other in a]{.s-t1} 

[liquid market for the security. And some students]{.s-t1} 

[then paid price A+B because they hoped to unload]{.s-t1} 

[on other students at a higher price before the day was over. What I will now confidently predict is]{.s-t1} 

[that, despite Camerer\'s experimental outcome,]{.s-t1} 

[most economics and corporate finance professors]{.s-t1} 

[who still believe in the \"hard-form efficient market hypothesis\" will retain their original belief. If so,]{.s-t1} 

[this will be one more indication of how irrational]{.s-t1} 

[smart people can be when influenced by psycho]{.s-t1} 

[logical tendencies.]{.s-t1} 

 

[My sixth question is: Don\'t moral and prudential problems come with knowledge of these]{.s-t1} 

[psychological tendencies? The answer is yes.]{.s-t1} 

[For instance, psychological knowledge improves persuasive power and, like other power, it can be used for good or ill. Captain Cook once played a]{.s-t1} 

[psychology-based trick on his seamen to cause them to eat sauerkraut and avoid scurvy. In my opinion.]{.s-t1} 

[this action was both ethical and wise under the]{.s-t1} 

[circumstances, despite the deliberate manipulation]{.s-t1} 

[involved. But ordinarily, when you try to use your]{.s-t1} 

[knowledge of psychological tendencies in the artful]{.s-t1} 

[manipulation of someone whose trust you need, you]{.s-t1} 

[will be making both a moral and prudential error.]{.s-t1} 

 

[The moral error is obvious. The prudential error]{.s-t1} 

[comes because many intelligent people, targeted for]{.s-t1} 

[conscious manipulation, are likely to figure out what you are trying to do and resent your action.]{.s-t1} 

 

[My final question is: Aren\'t there factual and]{.s-t1} 

[reasoning errors in this talk The answer is yes, almost surely yes. \'the final revision was made from memory over about fifty hours by a man eighty-one]{.s-t1} 

[years old, who never took a course in psychology]{.s-t1} 

[and has read none of it, except one book on developmental psychology, for nearly fifteen years. Even so, I think the totality of my talk will stand up very]{.s-t1} 

[well, and I hope all my descendants and friends will]{.s-t1} 

[carefully consider what I have said. I even hope]{.s-t1} 

[that more psychology professors will join me in:]{.s-t1} 

[(1) making heavy use of inversion; (2) driving for a]{.s-t1} 

[complete description of the psychological system so]{.s-t1} 

[that it works better as a checklist; and (3) especially]{.s-t1} 

[emphasizing effects from combinations of psychological tendencies.]{.s-t1} 

 

[Well that ends my talk. If in considering what I]{.s-t1} 

[have said yo U had ten percent the fun I had saying]{.s-t1} 

[It you were lucky recipients.]{.s-t1} 

 

[Talk Eleven Revisited]{.s-t4} 

 

[In this talk, made in 2000, I gave favorable mention to Judith Rich Harris strong-selling book The Nurture Assumption. You will recall that this work]{.s-t1} 

[demonstrated that peer pressure on the young is far more important, and]{.s-t1} 

[parental nurture is much less important, than had been commonly recognized. The success of the book, with its vast practical implications, has an interesting story behind it: Long before the book was published, Ms. Harris, now 67, was kicked out]{.s-t1} 

[of Harvard\'s PhD. program in psychology because Harvard believed that she lacked]{.s-t1} 

[qualities ideal in psychological research. Then, later, out of illness and obscurit_v, as she was pretty much housebound throughout adult life by unfixable autoimmune]{.s-t1} 

[disease, she published an academic paper on which her subsequent book was based.]{.s-t1} 

[And for that paper she won a prestigious medal, named after the man who signed]{.s-t1} 

[her dismissal notice from Harvard, awarded annually by the American Psychological]{.s-t1} 

[Association for distinction in published writing.]{.s-t1} 

 

[When I learned from her impressive book that this ironic result had occurred, I wrote to Harvard, my alma mater, urging it to award Ms. Harris, whom I did not know, an honorary PhD., or, better yet, a real Ph. D. I cited the example of Oxford. That great university once allowed its best student, Samuel Johnson, to leave without a degree because he was too poor to continue paying tuition. But Oxford later made gracious amends. It gave Johnson a doctorate after he conquered sickness and became famous in a tough climb once described in his own words: \"Slow rises worth, by poverty oppressed.\" I failed utterly in my effort to convince Harvard to imitate Oxford in this way. But Harvard did later recruit from MIT one of the most famous living psychology professors, Steven Pinker, and Pinker is a big admirer of Ms. Harris. From this step, we can see one reason why its liberal arts division is more highly regarded than most others. The division\'s extreme depth often allows partial correction of bonehead errors that would flourish unopposed elsewhere.]{.s-t1} 

 

[Judith Rich Harris (b. 1938)]{.s-t1} 

[Judith Rich Harris is an independent investigator and author. Her significant professional accomplishments include a mathematical model of visual speech, textbooks in developmental psychology, and many influential professional articles. She is best known for The Nurture Assumption (1998) and No Two Alike (2000. Ms. Harris lives with her husband in New Jersey.]{.s-t1} 

 

[In 2006, MS Harris, struggling further through her unfixable illness, has published another book, No two alike the title is apt because one Central question the author assaults is why identical twins turn out to be so different in important aspects of personality her dogged curiosity and Raigad in dealing with this question remind me of board Darwin and Sherlock Holmes and her solution is very plausible as she collects and explains data from Professional literature including an interesting case where in one of two identical twins became a success in business and family life while the other twin went to Skidrow.]{.s-t15} 

[I won\'t hear disclose MS Harris desirably generalised answer to her Central question because it would be better for eliminate readers to first guess the answer then read her book if miss Harris is roughly right which seems very likely to me she\'ll has twice from a very handicapped position produced academic and sides of great practical importance in child rearing, education and much else.]{.s-t15} 

 

[ how could this rare and desirable result happen? Well, by miss Harris own account, she was impertinent and skeptical even as a child. And these qualities + patient, determined skill have obviously served her truth seeking well, all the way through to age 67 no doubt she was also assisted by her enthusiasm in destroying her own ideas as she now demonstrate by apologizing for her former work as a text book writer who repeated wrong notions now outgrown]{.s-t15} 

 

[In this talk I displayed some impertinency of my own by delivering an extreme sounding message it claims nothing less than (1) that academic psychology is hugely important, (2) that even so it is usually ill thought out and ill presented by its PHD denizens and (3) that my way of presenting psychology often has a large superiority in practical utility compared to most textbooks. Naturally I believe these extreme claims are correct after all I assemble the material contained in this talk to help me succeed in practical thinking and not to gain advantage by making public any would be clever notions.]{.s-t15} 

 

[If anyone partly right the world will eventually see more Psychology in roughly the form of this talk if so I confidently predict that the change in practice will improve general competency.]{.s-t15}

["]{.s-t2}[A word to the wise is enough" - Poor Richard]{.s-t4} 

 

[The model for Poor Charlie\'s Almanack is, of course, Ben Franklin\'s Poor Richard\'s Almanac. Franklin, as many know, was a polymath. Born in Boston and a leader of the American Revolution, he was a journalist, publisher, author, philanthropist, abolitionist, public servant, scientist, librarian, diplomat, and inventor. Using the pseudonym of \"Poor Richard,\" Franklin published his Almanach from 1733 to 1758. Its content varied, including not only many Franklin aphorisms that became famous but also calendars, weather forecasts, astronomical information, and astrological data. The Almanac was hugely popular in the American colonies, selling about 10,000 copies per year.]{.s-t1} 

[Poor Richard\'s maxims ranged widely in topic and were typically laced with humor. Some samples include:]{.s-t1} 

[\"No nation was ever ruined by trade.\"]{.s-t1} 

[\"Drive the Business, or it will drive thee.\"]{.s-t1} 

[\"He that falls in love with himself will have no rivals.\"]{.s-t1} 

[\"Where there\'s Marriage without Love, there will be Love without Marriage. \"]{.s-t1} 

[\"Necessity never made a good bargain.\"]{.s-t1} 

[\"Three may keep a secret, but two of them are dead.\"]{.s-t1} 

[\"There is no little enemy.\"]{.s-t1} 

[\"It\'s difficult for an empty stack to stand upright.\"]{.s-t1}
